Doc 7 · Inflation-linked-Financial-Markets-RBA-Bulletin-Oct-2024.txt
RBA Bulletin: Inflation-linked Financial Markets (Oct 2024)
9,367 tokens · 40,091 characters
------------------------------------------------------------------------

Nick Baker, Dominique D’Netto, Mac McKenna and Dmitry Titkov* 

Photo: Andriy Onufriyenk – Getty Images 

Abstract 
Financial instruments with returns that are indexed to inflation allow market participants to hedge against or take positions on future inflation. Inflation-linked bond and swap markets in Australia are small and not very liquid relative to some other advanced economies. Nevertheless, pricing in these markets can provide valuable information about participants’ inflation expectations. Market measures of long-term inflation expectations have increased in many advanced economies since the COVID-19 pandemic. In Australia, this has brought expectations into closer alignment with the RBA’s inflation target. 

Introduction 
Inflation-linked financial instruments involve cash flows between market participants (for an inflation-linked bond, from the issuer to the holder) that depend on the rate of inflation. Inflation-linked markets serve two important purposes. First, they can be used for hedging. Market participants with inflation-linked assets, such as governments or infrastructure providers, can issue or enter into inflation-linked financial instruments with participants with inflation-linked liabilities, such as super funds or insurance companies, to reduce risks from future inflation. Second, inflation-linked markets reveal 

information about participants’ inflation expectations. 
This is because participants can profit by trading in these markets if their forecasts for inflation turn out to be more accurate than those of others. 

The two key types of inflation-linked financial instruments are capital indexed bonds (‘indexed bonds’) and inflation swaps.1 Pricing in inflation-linked markets provides measures of the compensation that market participants demand for holding an instrument that exposes them to future inflation. 
This is called ‘inflation compensation’ and can differ

Inflation-linked financial instruments What are they? 
Indexed bonds are a debt security in which the issuer promises to adjust principal and interest (or coupon) payments in line with a reference inflation index on a pre-determined frequency. The coupon is paid on the inflation-adjusted principal value to maintain the real value of the coupon, so the value of the payments to the holder of the security is constant in real terms because it varies directly with inflation outcomes over the life of the bond. This means the yield on an indexed bond is a real yield. Nominal bonds, in contrast, have fixed face values and coupon payments and therefore they provide a constant nominal cash flow and the yield on these bonds is in nominal terms. The inflation rate that equalises the expected return on equivalent maturity indexed and nominal bonds is known as the ‘breakeven’ inflation rate and is a measure of the inflation compensation demanded by participants in the bond market. 
As discussed in ‘Risk premia’ below, measures of inflation compensation can differ from market participants’ inflation expectations, including because participants can demand additional compensation for the risk that inflation turns out to be different from what they had expected. 

I N F L AT I O N - L I N K E D   F I N A N C I A L   M A R K E T S

from market participants’ expectations for future inflation, as discussed below. For swaps, inflation compensation can be inferred directly from swap pricing. For bonds, inflation compensation is inferred by comparing the pricing of indexed bonds with nominal bonds that have similar terms to maturity. 
Most advanced economies, including Australia, have markets for both inflation-linked bonds and swaps. Relative to some other advanced economies, Australia’s markets for indexed bonds and inflation swaps are modest in size and somewhat less liquid, particularly for swaps. 

Measures of inflation compensation are influenced by risk premia and technical factors, which can cloud their interpretations. This article explains these influences, before presenting estimates that attempt to decompose inflation compensation into components for inflation expectations and risk premia. Understanding how inflation-linked markets function, and decomposing inflation compensation, supports clearer interpretations of how consistent market participants’ inflation expectations are with central bank inflation targets. This article investigates market participants’ inflation expectations across advanced economies during the most recent inflationary shock – the COVID-19 pandemic. Inflation compensation fell at the onset of the pandemic across advanced economies before rising alongside the surge in inflation (Graph 1). Once estimates of risk premia are removed, pricing in inflation-linked markets suggests that participants’ long-term inflation expectations have settled around central bank inflation targets in most advanced economies as inflation has declined. In Australia, participants’ long-term inflation expectations are now anchored around the midpoint of the RBA’s inflation target (RBA 2024).

indexed bonds in 1989–1992 and 2004–2008 (McCray 1997). Over the past few years, Canada and Germany have ceased issuance completely. 

Indexed bonds account for a small share of total government debt in most economies (Graph 2). 
In Australia, Canada and Germany, indexed bonds comprise less than 5 per cent of total debt. 
These shares have fallen in most advanced economies since the pandemic, in part because governments relied on the more liquid nominal bond market to fund pandemic responses. While less than 10 per cent of US federal debt is in indexed bonds, the market is still large in absolute terms, with around US$2 trillion outstanding. Indexed bonds make up around a quarter of total government debt in the United Kingdom and Sweden.

Inflation swaps are a type of derivative in which two counterparties agree to exchange cash flows for a given notional amount and specified period, where the cash flows in one direction are linked to inflation. One counterparty pays a fixed interest rate, which is agreed at the initiation of the contract, in exchange for a floating interest rate that is based on the change in a reference inflation index. The fixed payment leg of the contract is a measure of the inflation compensation demanded over the term of the swap by participants in the swap market, as that is the rate at which market participants can agree to exchange future fixed and floating cash flows. 

Where did they come from? 
Indexed bonds 
There are several reasons why governments have issued indexed bonds (see, generally, Cole and Schaper 2024). These bonds can reduce the expected cost of debt servicing in so far as investors are willing to pay a premium to reduce their inflation risk (for more detail, see ‘Risk premia’ below). Indexed bonds may also allow governments to better hedge their net cash flows – at least in the short run – because when inflation is high, nominal tax receipts tend to increase immediately while spending tends to increase with a lag (Bankowski et al 2023). Issuing indexed bonds can help to reinforce anchored inflation expectations and monetary policy credibility by reducing the incentive for governments to inflate away the real value of their liabilities. A liquid indexed bond market creates a real risk-free benchmark rate for the economy (i.e. a rate free from both credit and inflation risk). Also, indexed bonds can improve the resilience of sovereign funding by increasing the diversity of the investor base. 

The earliest recorded use of indexed bonds was by the Commonwealth of Massachusetts in 1780 (Fisher 1913; Shiller 2003). In 1945, Finland became the first sovereign issuer of indexed bonds. Further sovereign issuance was initially limited to small economies with high inflation that struggled to issue nominal bonds in their own currencies (Garcia and van Rixtel 2007). 
In 1981, the United Kingdom became the first major advanced economy to issue an indexed bond. 
Other advanced economies followed later: Australia in 1985; Canada, Sweden, New Zealand and the United States in the 1990s; and Japan and Germany in the 2000s. However, issuance of indexed bonds has often been sporadic. For example, Australia stopped issuing 


-----

cash flows in inflation swaps), which they use to hedge their inflation-linked liabilities. Because investors in indexed bonds typically ‘buy and hold’ the asset for hedging purposes, liquidity in indexed bond markets can be significantly lower than in nominal bond markets, which tend to be more actively traded by a diverse range of participants. 
In Australia, domestic non-bank financial institutions – including super funds, insurance companies and fund managers – make up around half of the turnover in inflation-linked Australian Government securities (AGS; Graph 4). 

By contrast, Australian dollar inflation swap activity is dominated by international banks on both sides of the swap, though domestic non-banks are relatively more active on the side that receives inflation-linked cash flows. There are financial market participants, such as hedge funds, that may choose to pay or receive cash flows linked to inflation for speculative or diversification purposes, though this is primarily at shorter horizons. As a result of the interaction of supply and demand dynamics for these instruments, indexed bonds are typically only issued at longer maturities whereas inflation swaps tend to trade at maturities of between one and 30 years.

Inflation swaps 
Inflation swap markets emerged in the 1980s and 1990s alongside the issuance of indexed bonds, although they were initially small and non-standardised. The market for inflation swaps in Australia developed during the mid-2000s, as they provided an alternative to indexed bonds, the issuance of which was suspended temporarily by the Australian Government due to budget surpluses. 
Data on the size of inflation swap markets was, until recently, very limited as inflation swaps are an over-the-counter product. However, central clearing of inflation swaps in a number of overseas markets has increased considerably since 2016 and has been associated with an increase in trade volumes (Graph 3).2 By contrast, Australian dollar inflation swaps are not centrally cleared. Activity in the Australian inflation swap market is generally low and sporadic, and the size of the market has declined over recent years in terms of notional value outstanding. 
It is possible that activity could increase if clearing was offered – for example, if clearing drew in institutions that were either unable or reluctant to participate in an uncleared market.

Who trades them? 
The supply of indexed bonds is determined largely by government debt management decisions. Entities with revenues tied to inflation and low variable costs, such as infrastructure providers or utility companies, may also issue indexed bonds to hedge their inflation-linked assets or revenues. Super funds and insurance companies are the key sources of demand for indexed bonds (and for receiving inflation-linked 


-----

an investor would still receive the full par amount at maturity.8 See Appendix A for a comparison of design features of indexed bonds and inflation swaps across selected advanced economies. 

Risk premia 
Ideally, measures of inflation compensation would provide a direct view of market participants’ expectations for inflation. This would be the case if inflation-linked markets were efficient and frictionless and participants were risk-neutral. However, in practice, risk premia and the design features of indexed bonds and inflation swaps can influence measures of inflation compensation such that they do not reflect participants’ inflation expectations alone. 

Market participants may demand premia for inflation, liquidity and credit risk. These premia are difficult to measure, but models that decompose inflation compensation into inflation expectations and risk premia suggest that they can vary substantially over time. Inflation compensation and risk premia can differ across indexed bond and inflation swap markets due to differences between the two markets. 
While the spread between inflation swap rates and breakeven inflation rates implied by bond yields varies over time and across advanced economies, swap rates generally tend to be higher than breakeven rates (Graph 6). This spread has averaged around 20–30 basis points in recent years in Australia and some other advanced economies.

How do their design features differ across advanced economies? 
Indexed bonds and inflation swaps typically reference the same inflation index within an economy, although the index used varies across economies. In most cases, including Australia, the reference index is identical to the central bank inflation target. 
Where the index is different, measures of inflation compensation can differ from the inflation target (Graph 5). However, index spreads are typically material only in the United States and United Kingdom.3 
One difference between Australia and other advanced economies (except New Zealand) is that Australia’s reference index, the Consumer Price Index (CPI), is published at a quarterly rather than monthly frequency. The Australian Bureau of Statistics (ABS) plans to transition to a complete monthly CPI in late 2025, though it will continue to publish a quarterly CPI series.4 The monthly CPI will not automatically become the new reference index for inflation-linked financial instruments in Australia, given that the pricing formulae for them currently refer to the quarterly CPI.5 
There are other characteristics that can, at the margin, affect the pricing of inflation-linked financial instruments. First, the frequency of coupon payments can matter.6 Second, indexation lags can affect interpretations of inflation compensation, particularly at shorter horizons.7 Third, some indexed bond issuers (including the United States, Australia, Germany and Japan) offer deflation floors at maturity, so that if deflation drives the principal amount below par,

Credit risk 
Indexed bonds and inflation swaps can incorporate some degree of credit risk. For bonds, this is the risk that the issuer will default on coupon payments or the repayment of principal, while for swaps it is the risk that the counterparties to the swap are unable to meet their obligation to pay the fixed or the floating leg. As indexed bond breakeven rates are derived from the difference between the yield on nominal and indexed bonds, any credit risk is netted out, so credit risk premia are zero in breakeven rates. 
By contrast, inflation swap rates may incorporate some credit risk premia, with the magnitude varying depending on the credit risk of the counterparties involved. While collateralisation and central clearing can reduce credit risk, it may introduce other costs that affect pricing. Overall, credit risk may be contributing to the spread between inflation swap rates and indexed bond breakeven rates. 

I N F L AT I O N - L I N K E D   F I N A N C I A L   M A R K E T S

Inflation risk 
Future inflation is uncertain, so investors may demand compensation for the risk that inflation turns out to be higher or lower than their expectations. In theory, inflation risk premia could be positive or negative depending on the composition of participants in inflation-linked markets and their risk preferences, although estimates tend to be positive on average over time. This is consistent with greater demand for hedging inflation-linked liabilities than inflation-linked assets and may also reflect that unexpected inflation tends to hurt most market participants, so they are willing to pay a premium to insure against it. However, models decomposing inflation compensation into risk premia and inflation expectations should be treated with caution. Term structure models are commonly used, which assume nominal yields, real yields and inflation expectations are linear functions of ‘pricing’ factors, such as in Hambur and Finlay (2018). There are modelling limitations in decomposing market measures, particularly when the policy rate is at its effective lower bound, as it was in many advanced economies during the global financial crisis and the pandemic (Chung, Hui and Li 2017). 

Liquidity risk 
Measures of inflation compensation can incorporate liquidity premia, which compensates investors for market frictions in transacting in bonds or swaps. 
Indexed bonds are generally less liquid than nominal bonds, so liquidity premia are generally larger in indexed bond yields, which has the effect of reducing breakeven rates (Moore 2016). For inflation swaps, liquidity premia can be positive or negative depending on which side of the swap is more liquidity constrained. Dealer balance sheet constraints can also push up measures of inflation compensation from inflation swaps (Finlay and Olivan 2012). In the private sector, there is generally greater demand to receive inflation-linked cash flows than pay them, so market participants may require a premium to enter into swaps where they pay inflation-linked cash flows. Dealers may require a smaller premium to enter into swaps where there is a deeper and more liquid indexed bond market, because they can more easily hedge the swaps using the indexed bond market. 

Liquidity premia on inflation swaps and indexed bonds cannot be observed directly, but changes in liquidity premia can be inferred from movements in measures of market liquidity. Intraday yield movements – a measure of volatility that can be exacerbated by poor liquidity – tend to be similar in 


-----

Inflation compensation since the pandemic At the onset of the pandemic, inflation compensation declined considerably below central bank inflation targets, reaching record lows in Australia and the euro area, and the lowest levels since the global financial crisis in the United States (Graph 1). According to model estimates, inflation expectations declined a bit but generally remained closer to targets than inflation compensation, with the latter’s large declines driven mostly by risk premia (Graph 8; Burban et al 2021). 
In March 2020, pandemic-related uncertainty caused a sudden increase in liquidity demand (the so-called ‘dash for cash’) that saw liquidity premia become larger as bond holdings were widely sold off, with larger increases in less liquid markets.9 Because inflation-linked bond markets are less liquid than nominal bond markets, the liquidity premia demanded to hold them increased further, contributing to the decline in measures of inflation compensation. In Australia, poor liquidity continued to affect pricing in inflation-linked financial markets through to late 2020, though by 2021 these markets were once again functioning fairly well, as bid-offer spreads for inflation-linked bonds returned to around their pre-pandemic levels. Leaving aside risk premia, the decline in inflation expectations at the onset of the pandemic was consistent with the view that the pandemic would weaken the global economy, which helped prompt expansionary monetary policy (RBA 2020; FOMC 2020; Lagarde 2020). 

From mid-2020, short-term inflation compensation increased notably above central bank inflation targets, amid an increase in headline consumer price inflation.

I N F L AT I O N - L I N K E D   F I N A N C I A L   M A R K E T S

Inflation compensation returned to pre-pandemic levels around early 2021, before surpassing them in late 2021. This was several months before central banks started tightening monetary policy, suggesting market participants were quick to recognise building inflationary pressures. Short-term inflation swap rates reached record highs in many advanced economies in 2022. In Australia, the one-year inflation swap rate peaked at over 6 per cent in mid-2022, which was comparable to the RBA’s one-year-ahead forecasts for headline inflation around that time. Longer term rates experienced a smaller increase, indicating market participants did not expect high inflation to persist in the long term, partly because they expected central banks to respond to higher inflation in the near term by increasing their policy rates. 

According to model estimates, inflation expectations increased a bit in this period but again remained closer to central bank inflation targets than suggested by some measures of inflation compensation, as rising risk premia influenced these measures. Estimates of inflation risk premia moved higher, consistent with market participants perceiving an increase in uncertainty and upside risks to inflation, likely linked to supply chain pressures and high energy prices at the time (Lowe 2022; Lagarde 2022; Powell 2022). 
Additionally, in the United States, changes in liquidity premia pushed up inflation compensation as market functioning for indexed bonds improved. 

As central banks increased their policy rates quickly in 2022 to return high inflation to target and reduce the risk of above-target inflation becoming embedded in inflation expectations, long-term inflation expectations remained around targets. This suggests market participants expected central banks to set policy rates such that inflation would stay on target over the long term. Short-term measures of inflation expectations moderated from their peak, some of which reflected the unwinding of negative supply shocks in addition to tighter monetary policy settings. 

Over the past year, inflation compensation has settled above its pre-pandemic levels and is either consistent with or slightly above central bank inflation targets in the United States, euro area and Australia. 
Model estimates suggest long-term inflation expectations are generally anchored at targets.10 Risk premia have moved higher than their pre-pandemic levels in Australia and the euro area, and are little changed in the United States (Burban et al 2021; Lane 2024). In Australia, long-term inflation compensation drifted upward by around ½ percentage point 


-----

starting from early 2023, reflecting a rise in inflation expectations and inflation risk premia, though more so the latter. This upward drift brought long-term inflation expectations closer in line with the midpoint of the RBA’s inflation target, following a long period prior to and during the pandemic where they were below target (RBA 2024). 

Overall, the period since the pandemic highlights that inflation compensation in indexed bonds and inflation swaps contains factors beyond market participants’ inflation expectations, so caution is needed when interpreting these measures.11 

Nevertheless, because indexed bonds and inflation swaps price inflation compensation in real time, they are useful for policymakers as a timely indicator of market participants’ inflation expectations, to complement other, less timely, measures of inflation expectations such as surveys. 
After abstracting from risk premia, inflation expectations derived from pricing in inflation-linked markets generally support the view that long-term inflation expectations are well-anchored in advanced economies, including Australia.

The authors are from International Department and Domestic Markets Department. The authors would like to thank: the Derivatives Surveillance team at the Australian Securities and Investments Commission (ASIC) for processing and providing data on AUD inflation swaps; Andrew Barrelle from Barrenjoey for his insights on Australian inflation-linked financial markets; Matthew Wheadon from the Australian Office of Financial Management (AOFM) for clarifications regarding indexed bonds; and Susan Black, Jon Cheshire, Sean Dowling, Rachael Fitzpatrick, Matt Gibson, Jonathan Hambur, Callum Hudson, Christopher Kent, Jeremy Lawson, Gordana Peresin, Benn Robertson, Claudia Seibold and Penny Smith from the RBA for comments that improved the article. The cut-off for data used in this article is end August 2024 unless stated otherwise. 
This article focuses on capital indexed bonds, though there are other, less common types of inflation-indexed bonds, such as indexed annuity bonds. Indexed annuity bonds provide a fixed real stream of regular cash flows over the life of the bond, with no lump-sum principal repayment at the end of the loan period. 
Before the 2016 non-cleared margin rules came into effect, banks were hesitant to clear inflation swaps because this would have created a funding mismatch. 
The dealer would not have received initial margin from the client (on the non-cleared leg of the trade) but would have been required to post initial margin to the central counterparty. For a discussion of the costs and benefits of central clearing (in the context of bonds), see Cheshire and Embry (2023). 
From 2030, all new and outstanding indexed UK government bonds will be linked to the Consumer Price Index including housing costs (CPIH). The change in reference index is already influencing the pricing of indexed gilts maturing beyond 2030. 
Since September 2022, the ABS has published a monthly CPI indicator, which reflects updated prices for part of the CPI basket, whereas the complete monthly CPI will reflect updated prices for the whole CPI basket. 
For more detail, see AOFM (2023) for indexed bonds and Australian Financial Markets Association (2017) for inflation swaps.

10 

Inflation swaps are typically zero-coupon so there is only one payment made at maturity. Indexed bonds generally involve coupon payments and the frequency varies across markets. When there is a discrepancy in coupon frequency between the nominal and indexed bond (as in Australia and New Zealand), calculating an undistorted breakeven rate requires an adjustment to account for the compounding effect on yields. 
In most advanced economies, indexed bonds have an indexation lag of up to three months so that bonds traded between coupon dates can include accrued coupon payments from the previous coupon date. However, Australia and New Zealand have lags of up to six months (and sometimes longer) due to the quarterly publication of the CPI. Inflation swaps typically have a two- or three-month lag, so the floating leg payment is based on inflation over the period starting two to three months before the start date of the contract and ending two to three months before the termination date of the swap. 
There are also differences in how the lag is handled: daily interpolated lag or monthly interpolated lag. 
Without a deflation floor, the price of the indexed bond would decline below par value (and the yield would rise) if deflation occurred over the life of the bond, resulting in a lower implied breakeven rate. As a result, where the outlook is for low or no inflation, indexed bonds with a deflation floor can imply artificially breakeven rates (even if deflation is not the central expectation, as removing the risk of deflation can still imply artificially high breakeven rates). This is most relevant for Japan, where there is evidence the deflation floor has increased breakeven rates over time (Hiraki and Hirata 2020). 
For more detail on dysfunction in the Australian government bond market at the onset of the pandemic, see Finlay, Seibold and Xiang (2020). 
In the United States, this takes into account the average difference between the US Federal Reserve’s (Fed) inflation target and the reference rate used in inflation swaps and indexed bonds. US Treasury Inflation-protected Securities (TIPS) and most US dollar inflation swaps reference the Consumer Price Index for All Urban Consumers (CPI-U), whereas the Fed targets the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) index. The CPI-U has averaged 0.4 percentage points more than the PCE deflator over the past two decades.

This lesson is applicable outside of the post-pandemic period. In some economies, including Australia, the United States and euro area, there is evidence that changes in risk premia are a significant driver of changes in inflation compensation over time, and may be the 

dominant driver at times (D’Amico, Kim and Wei 2018; Finlay and Wende 2011; Böninghausen, Kidd and de Vincent-Humphreys 2018).

European Commission 
Directorate-General for Economic and Financial Affairs 

The Fiscal and Distributional Impact of the Reform of Unemployment Benefits in Belgium 

Kristine Van Herck, Savina Princen, Hannes Serruys and Arne Depoortere 

Abstract  
The employment rate in Belgium remains low, partly reflecting features of the tax and benefit system that weaken work incentives. More specifically, the benefit system in Belgium provided – until 1 January 2026 – unemployment  benefits  of  unlimited  duration,  which  made  Belgium  the  only  EU  Member  State  in  which unemployment  benefits  were  not  limited  in  time.  As  this  feature  may  have  discouraged  benefit  recipients from  taking  up  work,  the  federal  government  adopted  in  2025  a  long-awaited  reform  introducing  a maximum duration for unemployment benefits. Unemployment benefit recipients affected by the reform are expected  to  re–enter  employment,  while  the  most  vulnerable  long–term  unemployed  people  can  rely  on minimum income support.  
Based  on  micro-simulations  using  the  EUROMOD  model,  this  economic  brief  analyses  the  budgetary  and distributional effects of the unemployment benefit reform under different labour supply assumptions, taking into account the interaction  with the  minimum income support system. The analysis  finds that, even in the absence of labour supply effects, the reform is expected to generate net budgetary savings. However, when potential labour supply effects are taken into account, the net budgetary savings are significantly higher (up to  EUR  2.1  billion).  The  overall  impact  on  poverty  and  inequality  is  expected  to  be  limited  due  to  the cushioning effect of the minimum income support system and the potential increase in employment. 

JEL Classification: H53, J65, J68, D31, I38, C63. 

Keywords: Belgium, unemployment benefit reform, labour supply, budgetary savings, distributional effects, poverty, inequality, EUROMOD, tax-benefit microsimulation. 

Acknowledgements:  This  brief  benefited  from  valuable  comments  by  Kateryna  Bornukova  and  Cornelia Vandenberghe. 

Contact:  Savina  Princen, kristine.van– herck@ec.europa.eu, European Commission,  Directorate–General for Economic and Financial Affairs;  Hannes Serruys,  hannes.serruys@ec.europa.eu  and  Arne  Depoortere,  arne.depoortere@ec.europa.eu,  European Commission, Joint Research Centre. 

savina.princen@ec.europa.eu  and  Kristine  Van  Herck, 

EUROPEAN ECONOMY                                                                                                     Economic Brief 094                             

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


-----

## INTRODUCTION

Employment is comparatively low in Belgium, partially due to financial disincentives to work. 
The employment rate was 72.8% in 2025 (vs 76.1% on average in the EU), well below the 2030 national target  of  80%.  Low  employment  is  mainly  driven  by  a  low  employment  rate  among  disadvantaged groups, including people with low educational attainment, with a migrant background, with disabilities, or aged  over  60.  A  comparison  with  other  EU  Member  States  suggests  that  low  employment  can  be partially  explained  by  financial  disincentives  to  work.  Belgium  levies  high  labour  taxes  (social contributions  and  income  taxes)  compared  to  the  EU  average.  Moreover,  the  design  of  the  tax  and benefit system is complex and discourages recipients from taking up work or working more hours.1 To  help  address  this  labour  market  challenge,  the  federal  government  adopted  in  2025  a reform  of  the  unemployment  benefit  system.2 This  reform  limits  –  as  a  general  rule  ––  the maximum entitlement to unemployment benefits, which were unlimited in time until 1 January 2026, to maximum  two  years.  In  addition,  the  reform  limits  activation  benefits  for  young  people  who  have completed or ceased their studies (allocation d’insertion / inschakelingsuitkering) from three to one year. 
The reform of the unemployment benefit system is being gradually implemented since 1 January 2026. 
The  reform  aims  at  encouraging  unemployed  people  to  reintegrate  faster  in  the  labour market, although some of them are expected to rely on minimum income support. The federal government  expects  the  reform  to  restore  the  balance  between  social  contributions  and  benefits  to ensure  the  long–term  sustainability  of  the  social  security  system,  and  to  generate  some  budgetary savings. It also expects the reform to spur unemployment benefit recipients to take up work again and to permanently alter the labour market dynamics. It aims at “fundamentally reforming the unemployment benefit  system  into  a  genuine  insurance  system  […],  maximum  filling  open  vacancies,  increasing  the employment rate towards 80 % by 2029”3. The most vulnerable long–term unemployed people, however, are  expected  to  fall  back  on  the  minimum  income  support  system.  This  is  expected  to  reduce  the potential cost savings of the reform, but also to minimise its impact on poverty and inequality. 
This  economic  brief  aims  to  analyse  the  budgetary  and  distributional  effects  of  the  key feature  of  the  reform,  namely  limiting  unemployment  benefits  in  time.  In  the  analysis,  the interplay with other features of the benefit system, namely the uptake of minimum income support, will be studied based on micro-simulation using the EUROMOD model.4 In addition, also several scenarios as regards the impact of the reform on labour supply will be considered. The analysis is static in nature and therefore captures only the immediate “morning-after” effects of the reform, without taking into account longer-term behavioural responses beyond the labour supply scenarios considered. The note is organised as  follows.  The  first  section  describes  the  current  and  reformed  unemployment  benefit  system  in Belgium.  The  second  section  analyses  budgetary  and  distributional  effects  of  the  reform  based  on EUROMOD simulations, considering also potential labour supply effects. The final section concludes. 

1 A  long-standing  country  specific  recommendation  to  Belgium  is  therefore  to  “Reform  the  tax  and  benefits  system  to strengthen incentives to work by shifting the tax burden away from labour and by reviewing the design of benefits” Council Recommendation  of  21  October  2024  on  economic,  budgetary,  employment  and  structural  policies  of  Belgium (C/2024/6808). 
2 The programme law reforming the unemployment benefit system was adopted on 18 July 2025 and published in the Official Journal on 29 July 2025.  
3 Explanatory memorandum preparing the law: Ontwerp van programmawet van 27 mei 2025 / Projet de loi-programme du 27 mai 2025.  
4 See JRC Euromod website. 

 
 
 


-----

THE UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFIT SYSTEM 
ROLE AND DESIGN OF UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFITS  

Unemployment  benefits  protect  workers  from  income  loss,  support  job  search,  and  act  as automatic stabilisers during economic downturns. The main purpose of unemployment benefits is to provide income replacement in case of unemployment to avoid a significant deterioration in the living standards of unemployed people. By insuring workers against the risks linked to job loss, unemployment benefits provide a safety net for individuals and households. In addition, unemployment benefits operate as an automatic stabiliser within the macroeconomic framework: during economic downturns, increased benefit  payments  help  sustain  aggregate  demand,  while  in  periods  of  economic  expansion,  reduced reliance on unemployment benefits moderates public expenditure.  
The  duration  of  unemployment  benefits  plays  a  pivotal  role  in  balancing  social  protection with  labour  market  incentives.  Short  benefit  durations  can  limit  long-term  dependency  and encourage a rapid return to work, but they risk exposing individuals to income insecurity and poor job-matching, especially in economic downturns. Conversely, more generous durations provide unemployed workers with sufficient time to search for suitable employment and prevent poverty.5 Yet, long duration benefits can have a negative impact on labour supply. In the presence of relatively loose job search and availability  conditions  like  in  Belgium  (OECD,  2023),  incentive  problems  associated  with  moral  hazard may  lead  to  sub–optimal  job  search  intensity.,  This  may  result  in  longer  unemployment  spells,  which further  reduce  the  employability  of  jobseekers  (e.g.  Mortensen,  1997;  Shavell  and  Weiss,  1979). 
Furthermore,  unemployment  benefits  with  a  longer  duration  entail  a  significant  budgetary  cost  and increase  the  administrative  complexity  of  monitoring  compliance  with  job  search  and  availability requirements, as these need to be monitored over a longer period (e.g. Salvatori, 2022). 
In addition to the duration, also the time profile of the unemployment benefits is a relevant policy  parameter  for  the  design  of  the  benefit  scheme. In  general,  unemployment  benefits  are degressive  benefits,  meaning  that  the  income  replacement  rates  decline  over  time.  This  allows  for  a front-loaded  provision  of  income  support  at  the  start  of  the  unemployment  spell,  while  gradually reducing  the  relative  generosity  to  encourage  job  search  and  re-employment  (Cahuc  et  al.,  2014). 
However,  the  merits  of  this  design  have  been  contested  arguing  that  long-term  unemployed  people respond less to changes in benefit levels than short-term unemployed people as their chances of finding a  job  decline  with  the  duration  of  their  unemployment  spell,  irrespective  of  their  search  effort (Spinnewijn, 2015; Kolsrud et al., 2018; Campos et al., 2022). This suggests that degressivity should be more prominently deployed in the early stages of the unemployment spell instead of gradually declining over the spell.  
Finally, active search requirements and conditionality are also relevant to the incentives of jobseekers.  Empirical  research  demonstrates  that  mandatory  reporting  of  job  search  activities, participation  in  activation  programs,  and  proportionate  sanctions  for  non–compliance  significantly enhance re-employment outcomes (Boone et al., 2007). Integrating search requirements with degressive benefits  ensures  that  the  financial  incentive  created  by  declining  replacement  rates  reinforce  the jobseekers’ search behaviour. Furthermore, coupling these instruments with activation measures, such as job  counselling  and  training,  helps  mitigate  the  risk  of  long-term  unemployment,  particularly  for vulnerable groups. 

MAIN FEATURES OF THE UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFIT SYSTEM IN BELGIUM 

Until 2026, Belgium was the only EU Member State in which unemployment benefits were not limited  in  time.  This  feature  was  unique  in  the  EU  as  in  all  other  Member  States  unemployment 

5 Some argue that unemployment benefits that are unlimited in time allow jobseekers to search for better job matches as it  reduces  the  pressure  to  accept  the  first  available  job.  This  may  potentially  lead  to  higher  job  satisfaction,  higher productivity, and lower turnover (e.g. Cazes et al., 2016).

insurance benefits were limited in time (Eurofound, 2024). In  all  EU  Member States, except Belgium, the duration of benefits was limited in time to a maximum of two years in 2024.

Unemployment  benefits  are  an  individual  entitlement  in Belgium.  To  be  eligible,  a  person  must  have  lost  his/her  work  due  to  circumstances  beyond  his/her control.7 In  addition,  to  be  eligible  the  person  must  have  worked  a  certain  number  of  days  during  a certain  reference  period. In order to be eligible for unemployment benefits a job seeker must be actively looking for a job, although in Belgium these availability criteria are less stringent than in the neighbouring countries.  A  person  must  actively  seek  new  employment  by  registering  to  one  of  the  regional  Public Employment Services (PES), i.e. ACTIRIS in Brussels, FOREM in Wallonia VDAB in Flanders, or ADG in the German-speaking  Community. In  addition,  also  the  monitoring  of  the  job  search requirements  is  relatively  loose  in  Belgium  in  2022,  especially  as  regards  the  frequency  of  the monitoring  (European  Commission,  2025).  Belgium’s  unemployment  benefit  reform  does  not  alter  the availability criteria as such.

Unemployment  benefits  decrease  over  time  in  several  stages,  but  the  magnitude  of  the degressivity varies across  the level of the past wage and the household type. The amount of unemployment benefits received decreases in several stages over time, adding to the complexity of the benefit  system  and  potentially  undermining  its  signalling  function.9 During  the  first  three  months  of unemployment, benefits are calculated as 65% of the last wage received up to a ceiling. After this initial period,  the  benefits  decrease  gradually  over  several  months,  eventually  stabilising  at  a  lump-sum amount close to minimum income10. The amount of unemployment benefits depends on the last wage received,  the  family  composition  and  –  for  some  periods  –  also  on  the  employment  history  of  the jobseeker (Annex 1 Table A.2). The minimum income support is expressed per day under the assumption of 26 working days to ensure comparability with the unemployment benefit. 

THE 2026 REFORM OF THE UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFIT SYSTEM IN BELGIUM 

The  federal  government  significantly  reformed  the  unemployment  benefits,  limiting  their duration and reinforcing the link with the jobseeker’s employment history. The reform is being gradually  implemented  from  1  January  2026  onwards  and  limits  the  maximum  duration  of unemployment benefits to two years. Moreover, it changed the eligibility criteria by making the required employment history independent of the age of the person, except for some older workers (see below). 
The  reform  reinforced  the  link  between  the  duration  of  benefits  and  the  employment  history  of  the recipient:  when  a  person  has  worked  for  one  year  in  the  past  three  years,  the  person  is  entitled  to unemployment  benefits  for  one  year.  For  every  four  additional  months  of  work,  an  extra  month  of benefits is granted. Hence, a person who has worked five years is entitled to the full two–year benefit period. Also, activation benefits for young people who have completed or ceased their studies (allocation d’insertion / inschakelingsuitkering) were reduced in time from three to one year.11 

11 In addition, the reform reduced the mandatory period that a school-leaver must wait before becoming eligible for the unemployment benefit from approximately one year (310 days) to 6 months (156 days).  

 
 
 
 


-----

Some unemployed  people  are exempted  from  this  limitation  in  time. Older workers (individuals aged 55 and over), who worked at least 30 years (with a minimum of 156 days per year) are exempted from the time limitation. This threshold will increase to 35 years by 2030, tightening access over time. 
Furthermore,  some  sectoral  exceptions  apply  for  artists,  dock  workers,  and  sea  fishers  due  to  the irregular  nature  of  their  employment.  Moreover,  jobseekers  taking  a  training  course  for  critical  health care functions will continue to benefit from unemployment support beyond the two years’ duration. As a transitional measure, also unemployed people who started a training for a shortage profession in 2025 remain  eligible  for  unemployment  benefits  during  their  training,  even  when  their  total  unemployment spell exceeds two years. 

SPILLOVERS BETWEEN THE UNEMPLOYMENT REFORM AND THE MINIMUM INCOME  

Some  unemployed  individuals  who  lose  their  entitlement  to  unemployment  benefits  may qualify for minimum income support provided they meet a number of eligibility requirements. 
Eligibility  criteria  for  income  support  (leefloon  /  revenu  d’intégration  sociale)  relate  to  household composition,  age,  nationality,  residence,  lack  of  financial  resources,  willingness  to  work  and  having exhausted  rights  to  other  benefits.  The  gross  monthly  amount  of  minimum  income  (not  subject  to taxation  –  on  the  date  of  1  March  2026) is  EUR 893,67  per person for  eligible  co–habiting  household members,  EUR  1.340,47  for  a  single  person  and  EUR  1.811,57  for  a  person  with  a  dependent  family. 
These amounts may be combined with some labour income, with specific family benefits and tax credits, and with  other benefits  such  as  for  housing,  heating  and  social  assistance. By  limiting  the  duration  of the  unemployment  benefits  in  time,  some  unemployed  individuals  who  are  no  longer  eligible  for  an unemployment benefit are expected to turn to minimum income support as a fallback option.  
In principle, the reform may also generate spillovers to the disability scheme, as individuals with  health  limitations  who  experience  difficulties  in  meeting  the  availability  requirements may increasingly turn to disability benefits. However, eligibility for disability benefits is subject to a formal medical assessment, which limits the scope for additional inflows from unemployment. Moreover, since disability benefits are in most cases more generous than unemployment benefits, individuals who clearly qualify for disability support are likely to have already applied. Therefore, spillover effects with the disability scheme are not included in this economic brief. 

BUDGETARY AND DISTRIBUTIONAL EFFECTS OF THE 
UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFIT REFORM 
The  budgetary  and  distributional  effect  of  the  unemployment  benefit  reform  are  simulated using the EUROMOD model (Sutherland and Figari, 2013). The EUROMOD model allows to analyse the effects  of  tax  and  social  benefit  policy  reforms  on  household  incomes  across  EU  Member  States.  The model operates by applying country-specific tax and benefit rules to anonymised household data from the EU Statistics on Income and Living Conditions (EU-SILC).12 This allows to assess the budgetary and distributional  effects  of  reforms  as  compared  to  a baseline  scenario.  The reform  simulation  limits  the duration of unemployment benefits to two years for people aged 55 and below as well as those older than 55 years with an employment history of less than 30 years.13 (see Box 1 for more details on the modelling assumptions). 

12 Based on the 2024 EU SILC, the reform is likely to affect 89 099 individuals, which is well below the estimate provided by the government (100 000 beneficiaries affected). This difference may be due to the unemployment status being self-reported. In the survey some jobseekers may have reported a different economic status (e.g.: being in vocational training) or benefit type even though in reality they receive an unemployment benefit. This may lead to an underestimation in the total number of very long-term unemployed people in the EU SILC as compared to administrative data. Additionally, there may be differences in the length of the latest unemployment spell between official statistics of the National Employment Office and self-reported information. 
13 Due to data constraints, simulations do not take into account the minimum number of days per year required by law. 
Moreover,  as  result  of  data  constraints  some  features  of  the  reform  are  not  reflected  in  the  simulations,  namely  the 

 
 
 


-----

Four alternative labour supply scenarios are modelled through the EUROMOD Labour Market Adjustment  (LMA)  add-on. When losing unemployment benefits, part of those unemployed for more than two years will take up employment and for these workers a counterfactual wage will be set based on their individual and household characteristics (see Box 1). In addition, more recent unemployed (with an unemployment duration of less than two years) may also be motivated to take up employment faster when  they  face  the  prospect  of  losing  their  unemployment  benefit  after  two  years.  To  capture  these labour  supply  responses,  the  EUROMOD  Labour  Market  Adjustment  (LMA)  add-on  is  used  to  model transitions  in  employment  status.  The  LMA  add-on  implements  labour  supply  by  reassigning  labour market status for the relevant share of affected individuals and imputing associated earnings, months of  work,  and  hours  under  standardised  assumptions  (e.g.  wage  employment  rather  than  self-employment)  (see  Box  1).  Other  income  sources  tied  to  previous  labour  market  status  are  adjusted accordingly, and eligibility for benefits is recalculated. There are four alternative scenarios modelled for the labour supply effects, assuming an uptake of employment by, respectively, 10%, 20% and 33% of those that lose their unemployment benefit following the reform. The first two scenarios are selected ad hoc,  while  the  last  one  (33%) is  in  line  with  the  estimates  by  the  federal  government  that  one  out  of three individuals losing unemployment benefit entitlement is assumed to take up employment again. 
The  simulated  budgetary  and  distributional  effects  of  the  reform  also  reflect  the  interplay with  minimum  income  support.  Following  the  reform,  some  beneficiaries  who  lose  unemployment benefits  will  become  eligible  for  minimum  income  support.  However,  for  this  type  of  support  a beneficiary  needs  to  apply  as  it  is  not  granted  automatically.  As  the  EUROMOD  baseline  simulations assume full benefit take-up, they tend to overestimate the number of beneficiaries of minimum income. 
To account for this bias, the standard EUROMOD model for Belgium uses a ‘benefit take-up adjustment’ of minimum income support. In the description of the results, both simulations (full-take up and adjusted for  non-take  up)  are  presented  to  provide  an  upper  and  lower  bound  for  the  minimum  income simulation. 

BOX – EUROMOD MICRO-SIMULATIONS OF THE UNEMPLOYMENT BENEFIT REFORM 

Micro-simulation models  such as  EUROMOD allow to take into account the interactions between the reform element (limiting the unemployment benefits in time) and all other features of the tax and  benefit system. 
These interactions might have a non-trivial impact on work incentives, especially in countries such as Belgium with  well-developed  social  systems  (see  HOME  |  EUROMOD  -  Tax-benefit  microsimulation  model  for  the European Union for more details on the model). 
The simulation of limiting unemployment benefits in time is carried out in several steps: 

  Adjust EUROMOD input data so that unemployment benefits are set to zero if the person has been unemployed  for  24  months  or  longer  and  is  aged  55  years  or  below.  In  addition,  unemployment benefits are also set equal to zero for people older than 55 years if they have an employment history of less than 30 years.  

  Run  EUROMOD  with  the  adjusted  data.  In  this  simulation,  a  person  whose  unemployment  benefits have  been set to zero  might be assigned certain means-tested benefits for  which  he  would  not be eligible  while  receiving  unemployment  benefits,  depending  on  the  eligibility  rules  modelled  in EUROMOD’s  baseline.  In  this  way,  the  simulation  accounts  for  the  interaction  of  all  the  tax-benefit components,  including  the  indirect  effect  this  reform  has  on  social  insurance  contributions  and personal  income  taxes.  However,  due  to  data  limitations,  it  is  not  possible  to  model  all  eligibility criteria for the minimum income benefits. Eligibility for the minimum income benefit depends on both income and wealth of the individual (and of other members of the family or household). Since wealth is not reported in SILC data, the EUROMOD simulation only considers an estimate of wealth based on reported  income  from  assets.  Further,  the  EUROMOD  simulation  does  not  distinguish  between  the 

changes in the eligibility of workers without or with only a short employment history, including young people who never worked This may lead to an underestimation of the impact of the reform. 

 
 
 
 


-----

income and wealth from parents and cohabiting children, pooling them in the household income. As PCSWs have discretionary power to decide whether to include parents and/or children in the means test, this will lead to a downward bias in the number of minimum income beneficiaries. 

  Model  the  labour  supply  effect  (transitions  in  employment  status)  through  the  EUROMOD  Labour Market  Adjustment  (LMA)  add-on.  The  method  first  identifies  who  is  affected  by  the  reform. 
Subsequently,  a  sorting  by  alignment  method  is  used  to  identify  who  is  most  likely  to  switch  to employment.  This  method  uses  a  logistic  regression  model  to  predict  the  probability  of  being employed  based  on  socio-demographic  characteristics.  Then,  the  affected  individuals  are  sorted based  on  the  employment  probability  resulting  from  the  logistic  regression  model.  Accounting  for population  weights,  the  method  allows  to  identify  the  first  X%  of  individuals  who  would  switch  to employment  in  the  counterfactual  simulation.  The  LMA-addon  of  EUROMOD  will  switch  the employment  status  of  these  individuals  to  full-time  employment.  The  counterfactual  gross  hourly wage is part of the standard EUROMOD input data and calculated using a Heckman selection model. 

Following  the  unemployment  benefit  reform,  some  beneficiaries  who  lose  unemployment  benefits  will become eligible for minimum income support. While not all eligible individuals will request minimum income support, EUROMOD baseline simulations assume full benefit take-up (see EUROMOD country report provides more information on eligibility rules and EUROMOD take-up adjustment assumptions). To correct for this bias, the standard EUROMOD model for Belgium uses a ‘benefit take-up adjustment’ of minimum income support. 
In particular, the Belgian EUROMOD model adjusts for non-take-up with a random non-take-up correction by applying  the  take-up  proportion  as  estimated  by  the  TAKE  project  (https://www.belspo.be/belspo/brain-be/projects/FinalReports/TAKE_FinRep.pdf). 
The  default  benefit  take-up  adjustment  (BTA)  in  EUROMOD  uses  official  statistics  for  the  calibration  of random  assignments  of  minimum  income.  The  number  of  total  minimum  income  recipients  in  the  country simulated  without  take-up  adjustment  is  compared  with  the  actual  number  of  minimum  income  recipients based  on  official  statistics.  When  BTA  is  “on”,  the  simulation  of  minimum  income  is,  therefore,  somewhat anchored to the take-up of minimum income in the baseline policy scenario, which is 56% in this simulation. 
However,  removing  unemployment  benefits  for  the  long-term  unemployed  results  in  a  deviation  from  the baseline scenario because the number of minimum income recipients is increasing, which will likely affect the take-up probabilities. For this reason, the note presents the results of both simulations (BTA turned “off” and BTA turned “on”).  

## BUDGETARY EFFECT

Disregarding  labour  supply  effects,  the  unemployment  benefit  reform  leads  to  a  positive budgetary impact on public finances. The first three rows of Table 1 show the budgetary effects of limiting  unemployment  benefits  to  a  maximum  of  two  years,  both  without  and  with  adjustments  for take-up  of  minimum  income  support,  in  the  absence  of  labour  supply  effects.  Simulations  show  that reducing  unemployment  spending  annually  increases  the  net  budgetary  position  of  the  government between  EUR  355.4  million  (lower  bound,  when  full  take-up  of  minimum  income  support  is  assumed) and  EUR  838.9  million  (upper  bound,  with  take-up  adjustments),  reflecting  direct  taxes  and  social contributions  net  of  cash  transfers.  The  decrease  in  unemployment  benefit  expenditure  is  substantial (EUR 1 334 million) and more than compensates for the increase in minimum income support (EUR 890 million  assuming  full  take-up  and  EUR  407  million  with  adjusted  take-up).  The  decrease  in unemployment  benefit  expenditure  also  compensates  for  the  decrease  in  direct  tax  revenues  (EUR  71 million) when labour supply effects are not considered. This tax revenue loss is explained by the fact that unemployment benefits are subject to personal income taxes, while minimum income support is not. 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 


-----

Graph 4. Estimated budgetary savings for different labour supply scenarios under the adjusted benefit take-up assumption (mio EUR) 

Source. Estimated results based on EUROMOD J1.164, policy system 2025, data from EU SILC 2024.  

Note: The category “other benefits” include child benefits and birth allowances.  

When  labour  supply  effects  are  simulated,  the  positive  budgetary  impact  of  the  reform increases substantially. The remaining rows of Table 1 present the full implementation impact of the reform  allowing  for  labour  supply  effects  under  three  alternative  labour  supply  scenarios.  The  last scenario  (33%)  is  based  on  the  assumptions  used  by  the  Belgian  government  that  one  out  of  three individuals affected by the reform would take up employment.  14 Considering this scenario, the positive fiscal  impact  is  expected  to  increase  to  between  EUR  1 785.6  million  (full  benefit  take-up)  and  EUR 
2 107.7  million  (adjusted  benefit  take-up).  The  additional  positive  budgetary  effect  compared  to  the simulations  without  labour  supply  effects  stems  mainly  from  increased  revenues  coming  from  direct taxes  and  social  security  contributions.  Labour  supply  effects  resulted  in  an  increase  of  EUR  402.6 million  in  direct  taxes  and  EUR  673.2  million  in  social  security  contributions.  In  addition,  there  is  a reduction of minimum income expenditure of EUR 228.9 million as compared to the simulation without labour supply effects and a EUR 21.3 million reduction of expenditures due to other benefits. Graph 4 visualises  the  budgetary  impact  for  the  different  labour  supply  scenarios  under  the  adjusted  benefit take-up  assumption.  These  findings  are  in  line  with  administrative  estimates  which  suggest  that  the reform will affect over 100 000 unemployed and – under the assumption that one third of them take up employment15 – it may yield budgetary savings up to EUR 900 million in 2026, rising to EUR 1.9 billion by 2029.16  

However,  the  simulations  suggest  that  the  magnitude  of  the  budgetary  effect  depends strongly  on  the  extent  to  which  unemployed  take  up  employment.  When  the  share  of unemployed  affected  by  the  reform  that  take  up  of employment  amounts  only  to  20%,  the  net  fiscal impulse declines to EUR 1 711.4 million (under the assumption of adjusted take up). The adjusted take-up rate accounts for the fact that not all individuals who are eligible for minimum income support actually claim it, correcting the full take-up assumption in the baseline EUROMOD simulations. The adjusted take-up rate in this simulation is 56% (see box for a more detailed description. The figure in brackets next to the budgetary impact expresses the budgetary impact as a share of the 2025 GDP.  
To  maximise  the  budgetary  impulse  stemming  from  the  reform,  the  authorities will  need  to step up their efforts to bring unemployed into the labour market. In 2024, the transition rate to employment for an unemployed with an unemployment spell of two years or more was 6% in Belgium. 
The unemployment benefit reform is likely to increase this transition rate to employment. Especially in the first months after the reform, a temporary spike in the transition rate is expected as some of the long-term  unemployed  will  face  a  strong  financial  incentive  to  find  a  job  as  they  will  not  qualify  for minimum income support. Preliminary data from the Flemish public employment services suggest that between 11% and 26% of long-term unemployed benefit recipients affected by the reform transitioned into  employment  after  losing  their  unemployment  benefits  after  the  implementation  of  the  reform.17 However,  while  this  figure  is  promising  it  remains  below  the  rate  of  33%  expected  by  the  Belgian government. This implies that to achieve an outflow of one out of three to employment, targeting active labour market support to those jobseekers, which are only weakly attached to the labour market, will be paramount.

## DISTRIBUTIONAL EFFECTS

In  the  scenario  without  labour  supply  effects,  limiting  unemployment  benefits  in  time  has only  a  small  negative  impact  on  household  incomes  as  the  adverse  income  effect  of  the reform  is  largely  offset  by  minimum  income  support.  In  this  scenario,  disposable  household incomes  decrease  across  entire  population  on  average  by  0.32%  with  respect  to  the  baseline.  The decline is strongest for the three lowest income deciles, ranging from –3.1% in the first income decile to –0.7% in the third income decile. The impact on the higher income deciles is even smaller (Graph 5). 
Graph 5: Impact of the simulated reform on disposable household income by decile 

Source: EUROMOD-based simulated results (J1.164).  

Note: Deciles of equivalised household income (modified-OECD scales). Policy system of 2025, data from SILC 2024. The results have been simulated with benefit take-up adjustment on. The simulation with the labour supply effects assumes a 33% transition rate to employment.  
When  labour  supply  effects  are  taken  into  account,  the  negative  impact  of  the  reform  on household incomes is even reversed, except for those in the lowest income decile. For all but the  first  income  decile,  the  simulation  results  show  that  the  switch  to  work  fully  offsets  the  negative income effect of the unemployment benefit reform, resulting in higher household incomes than before the reform. Considering labour supply effects, the reform results in an increase of 0.73% of disposable household  income  for  the  second  income  decile  (as  compared  to  -1.2%  without labour  supply  effects) and smaller, but still positive effects in the higher income deciles. 18 Only in the first decile, the effect of the reform on household income remains negative (–1.38%) although it is considerably smaller than in the scenario in which there are no labour supply effects (-3.1%).  
Minimum  income  support  particularly benefits  households  in  the  three  lowest deciles, while labour supply effects are more uniformly distributed across the income distribution. Graph 6 shows the shares of the affected individuals relative to the total population by income decile, that either 

18 The number of affected individuals per decile drops substantially in the third and higher deciles, reducing the precision of these estimates. 

 
 
 


-----

start receiving minimum income support, take up employment, or neither of those cases (‘other affected’ 
– not switched to employment nor eligible for minimum income support). According to the simulations, more  than  half  of  the  individuals  in  the  lowest  income  decile  losing  unemployment  benefits  would receive  minimum  income  support.  Around  15  %  of  those  individuals  would  start  to  work.  This corresponds to an increase of the employment rate by 0.37 pps.  

Graph 6: Shares of the affected individuals as a percentage of that decile’s population, by income decile 

Source. EUROMOD-based simulated results (J1.164).  

Note.  Shares  of  the  affected  individuals  as  percentages  of  total  population  in  each  decile  when  UB  is  removed.,  while taking into account labour supply effects. The  simulation  with  the  labour  supply  effects  assumes  a  33%  transition  rate  to employment. 
Overall,  the  reform  is  expected  to  lead  to  a  negligible  increase  in  income  inequality  and poverty.  In  the  scenario  without  labour  supply  effects,  the  Gini  coefficient  of  equivalised  household disposable  income  is  projected  to  increase  marginally,  from  0.222  to  0.225.  When  potential  labour supply  effects  are  taken  into  account;  assuming  that  33%  of  affected  individuals  transition  into employment, the change is even smaller, with the Gini coefficient reaching 0.223. The S80/S20 indicator (i.e. ratio of the shares of the top 20% with respect to the bottom 20%) is expected to increase slightly from 1.921 to 1.933 without labour supply effects and only to 1.923 when taking into account labour supply  effects.  Given  the  expected  decline  in  income  at  the  bottom,  the  at-risk-of-poverty  rate  (AROP, with  a  threshold  defined  at  60%  of  the  median)  would  increase  by  0.39  pp  from  15.52%  to  15.91% without  labour  supply  effects.  When  labour  supply  effects  are  taken  into  account,  assuming  a  33% transition rate into employment, the poverty rate is expected to remain broadly stable.  

 
 
 
 


-----

## CONCLUSIONS

In July 2025, the Belgian federal government adopted a reform of the unemployment benefit system  to  respond  to  the  labour  market  challenges  that  the  country  is  facing.  The  reform gradually  implemented  since  1  January  2026  consists  of  several  measures  of  which  the  main  one  is limiting  unemployment  benefits  to  two  years  for  those  younger  than  55  years  or  those  with  an employment history of less than 30 years. Some stakeholders have argued that the budgetary savings may be limited, as jobseekers who loose eligibility for unemployment support rely on minimum income support.  In  addition,  they  argued  that  the  reform  would  have  a  strong  negative  impact  on  poverty.  To assess these concerns this economic brief analyses, based on EUROMOD microsimulation, the budgetary and  distributional  effects  of  limiting  unemployment  benefits  in  time,  while  taking  into  account  the interaction with the minimum income support system. 
According  to  EUROMOD  simulations,  the  budgetary  savings  of  the  reform  would  range between EUR 355 and 2 107 million. In the absence of labour supply effects, the simulations indicate that the reform would generate between EUR 355 and 839 million savings, depending on the take-up of minimum  income  support.  The  reform,  however,  is  expected  to  incentivise  workers  to  take  up employment  and  when  considering  these  behavioural  responses,  the  budgetary  impact  increases substantially. Under the scenario in which one third of affected individuals transition into employment, net budgetary gains are estimated up to EUR 2.1 billion, driven by higher revenues from personal income taxes  and  social  security  contributions,  and  lower  expenditure  on  minimum  income  support.  These findings  suggest  that  the  labour  market  response  will  be  the  main  determinant  of  the  reform's  fiscal impact. 
The  distributional  impact  of  the  reform  is  expected  to  remain  limited.  Disposable  household income declines only modestly in the absence of labour supply effects, as the minimum income support system offsets a large part of the income loss among affected households. When labour supply effects are included, income losses are substantially reduced and are fully offset for almost all income groups. 
Consequently, the reform is projected to have only a marginal effect on inequality and poverty.  
The  reform  could  help  improve  fiscal  sustainability  while  maintaining  strong  income protection,  particularly  if  accompanied  by  effective  employment  support  measures.  While specific  targeted  measures  are  currently  in  place,  the  effectiveness  and  efficiency  of  these  measures vary  substantially  (Desiere  et  al.,  2010).  For  instance,  a  hiring  subsidy  for  long-term  unemployed  was found  to  have  a  significant  positive  impact  on  the  transition  out  of  unemployment,  but  this  effect  is mainly driven by higher educated participants which are probably the least vulnerable group among the long-term  unemployed  (Desiere  and  Cockx,  2022).  Given  the  substantial  loss  of  skills  during  a  long unemployment spell, up- and re-skilling measures, including on-the-job training are expected to be most effective  (e.g.  Cockx  et  al.,  1998;  Jacobson  et  al.,  2011).  Large  differences  in  socio-economic characteristics  among  the  very  long-term  unemployed  underlines  also  the  importance  of  personalised guidance  and  job  search  support.  In  addition,  early  intervention for  jobseekers  is warranted  to  prevent skills depreciation and social isolation.  
While  the  unemployment  benefit  reform  presents  a  necessary  step  toward  improving  work incentives and reducing long-term unemployment, it remains insufficient to achieve the 80% employment  target.  In  2024,  the  employment  rate  in  Belgium  reached  72.3%,  well  below  the  80% target  for  2030  and  even  in  case  zero  unemployment  is  assumed,  it  would  remain  below  the  target (76.4%).  This  is  linked  to  the  fact  that  Belgium  faces  a  structural  issue  that  extends  beyond  the registered  unemployed:  a  large  share  of  the  working-age  population  remains  outside  the  labour  force altogether.  Therefore,  activating  only  those  receiving  unemployment  benefits  will  not  be  enough  to substantially raise overall employment levels. A comprehensive activation strategy should encompass all inactive  groups,  combining  benefit  reform  with  measures  that  enhance  employability,  and  remove disincentives to participation across all areas of the welfare system.

The Yield and Market Function Effects of the Reserve Bank of Australia’s

The Yield and Market Function Effects of the 
Reserve Bank of Australia’s Bond Purchases 

## Richard Finlay, Dmitry Titkov and Michelle Xiang

Research Discussion Paper 
2022-02 

May 2022 

Domestic Markets Department 
Reserve Bank of Australia 

The authors would like to thank Matthew Boge, Andrea Brischetto, Christopher Kent, Marion Kohler, Carl Schwartz, John Simon, Matthew Wheadon and participants in internal Reserve Bank seminars for helpful comments and suggestions. The views expressed in this paper are those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect the views of the Reserve Bank of Australia. The authors are solely responsible for any errors. 

Corresponding Author: finlayr at domain rba.gov.au 

External Communications: rbainfo@rba.gov.au 

https://doi.org/10.47688/rdp2022-02 

 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 


-----

## Abstract

We  examine  the  effect  on  government  bond  yields  of  three  Reserve  Bank  of  Australia  policy measures implemented following the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic. We also assess the impact of  the  three  measures  on  government  bond  market  functioning.  The  three  measures  were: 
purchases to support government bond market function over early 2020; the yield target on 3-year Australian government bonds; and the bond purchase program to lower longer-term yields from late 2020 until early 2022. For purchases to support market function, we find that the announcement lowered short-dated Australian Government Securities (AGS) yields, but did not lower longer-dated AGS yields. We also find that such purchases led to lower yields as and when they were implemented, and that they supported market function by lowering bid-offer spreads. For the yield target, we find a substantial announcement effect and moderate implementation effects on yields. Conversely, the yield  target  appears  to  have  detrimentally  affected  some  aspects  of  government  bond  market function. For the bond purchase program, we find an announcement effect of around 30 basis points for longer-term AGS yields, while any implementation effects were small and temporary. 

JEL Classification Numbers: E52, E58, G12 
Keywords: market function, yield target, quantitative easing, event study
